--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 1 Results

The top 7 machines all scored 0.00, which is the best possible. The champion is a trivially simple 1-state Stop machine. This is surprising - robots that stop completely score 0.00? 

Wait - thinking about this more carefully: if all robots stop, they deliver no items, so the score stays at 0. The scoring rule says delivering a good item decreases score by 1, delivering a bad item increases it by 1. So a stopped robot scores 0.00 (no deliveries at all).

The goal is to MINIMIZE the score. So we want to:
1. Deliver items from the BENEFICIAL half (decreases score by 1 each)
2. Avoid delivering items from the HARMFUL half (increases score by 1 each)

A score of 0.00 means net deliveries = 0, which could be all stopped, or equal good and bad deliveries. Negative scores would be better!

The machines scoring 0.07, 0.12, 0.21, 0.60, 0.62, 0.72 are worse - they're delivering more bad items than good items.

So the real challenge is: can we get negative scores by consistently delivering good items? We need to identify which half (BLUE or GREEN walls) is beneficial. The problem states "an item from one half decreases the score by 1" - we need to navigate toward the correct color.

### Strategy
- **GoToColor** toward BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) walls to pick up items from that half
- Return to nest (WHITE floor) to deliver
- Use floor color to detect nest (WHITE) and source (BLACK)

Key design:
- State: Go to source (GoToColor toward a colored wall)
- When on BLACK floor (source), go toward nest (GoAwayColor from source wall, or GoToColor toward RED wall)
- When on WHITE floor (nest), deliver and go back

Let me try designs that navigate to ONE specific color source (either BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) and return to nest (RED wall=1, or go away from source).

Since we don't know which color is beneficial, I'll try both BLUE and GREEN, plus some exploration.

The best FSMs should:
1. Go toward one color source (BLUE walls clr=3 or GREEN walls clr=2)
2. When on black floor (picked up item), head to nest (toward RED wall clr=1, or away from source)
3. When on white floor (delivered), go back to source

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **SURVIVAL**: Copy champion (Stop, score 0.00)
2-7: **Exploitation** of simple structures with GoToColor to one source + return
8-14: **Exploration** of different topologies

For the foraging mission, a key insight: robots need to distinguish BLUE vs GREEN source. Let me try going to BLUE (clr=3) source specifically:

State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), when on BLACK floor → go to nest
State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1) = nest wall, when on WHITE floor → go to source

Also try GREEN (clr=2):
State 0: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2), when on BLACK → go to nest
State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1), when on WHITE → go back

## Reasoning

### Key Insights from Round 2

The champion scores -13.67 with:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2), transitions back to state 0 on BLACK floor (p=0.95) or GRAY floor (p=0.7)
- State 1: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), transitions back to state 0 on WHITE floor (p=0.9) or GRAY floor (p=0.3)

**Critical finding**: GREEN walls (clr=2) is the BENEFICIAL source (decreases score). The robot goes toward GREEN to pick up items, then flees from GREEN to return to nest, and when it detects white floor (nest), goes back to GREEN.

Wait - both transitions from state 0 go back to state 0 (destination 0 = other state = state 1? No wait...). With 2 states, from state 0, `--n0x0 0` means destination index 0 among OTHER states = state 1. So:
- State 0 transitions: on BLACK → go to state 1; on GRAY → go to state 1
- State 1 transitions: on WHITE → go to state 0; on GRAY → go to state 0

So: GoToColor GREEN (pick up at black) → GoAwayColor GREEN (flee toward nest) → repeat when at white/gray.

The GoAwayColor GREEN makes robots flee the green area, which means they move toward the nest (opposite side). Smart!

BLUE (clr=3) is the HARMFUL source. Machine #2 with BLUE scored -11.40 (also good but worse).

### Strategy for Round 3

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Tune probabilities of champion, add more transitions, try slight variations
3. **Exploration**: Try GoToColor GREEN + GoToColor RED (nest wall), 3-state versions, different transition probabilities

Key variables to tune:
- p0x0 (BLACK→flee): try 0.99, 0.85, 0.9
- p0x1 (GRAY→flee): try 0.5, 0.3, 0.8, 0.9
- p1x0 (WHITE→go): try 0.95, 0.99, 0.85
- p1x1 (GRAY→go): try 0.1, 0.2, 0.5

Also try: GoToColor GREEN + GoToColor RED (wall color 1) for return to nest.

3-state: GoToColor GREEN → GoAwayColor GREEN → (explore/wait) → back

Note: clr values: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. LED colors: 0,4,5,6 only.

The top 9 machines all scored between -12.76 and -14.22, forming a tight cluster. The champion is:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2), 3 transitions: BLACK→state1 (p=0.95), GRAY→state1 (p=0.7), random→state1 (p=0.01)
- State 1: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), 2 transitions: WHITE→state0 (p=0.9), GRAY→state0 (p=0.3)

Key insights:
1. The 2-state GoToColor GREEN / GoAwayColor GREEN pattern is very strong
2. The GRAY transition (p=0.7) in state 0 helps escape the gray zone toward source
3. Adding a tiny random transition (p=0.01) slightly helps
4. 3-state designs with extra exploration states performed worse (scores -0.83 to -1.15)
5. Using GoToColor RED (clr=1) for return worked somewhat (-3.63) but worse than GoAwayColor GREEN

The big gap is between rank 9 (-12.76) and rank 10 (-3.63), confirming the 2-state GREEN pattern dominates.

### Strategy for Round 4

**Exploitation** (most machines): Fine-tune the champion parameters:
- Vary p0x0 (BLACK→flee): 0.9, 0.95, 0.99, 1.0
- Vary p0x1 (GRAY→flee): 0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8, 0.9
- Vary p0x2 (random→flee): 0.005, 0.01, 0.02, 0.05
- Vary p1x0 (WHITE→go): 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99
- Vary p1x1 (GRAY→go): 0.2, 0.3, 0.4, 0.5
- Try adding a 4th transition to state 0 (WHITE→state1 with low p)
- Try the 4-transition version from rank 2 with tweaks

**Exploration**: 
- Try adding a "perceive GREEN" camera condition to help detect source earlier
- Try 3-state with GoToColor GREEN → GoAwayColor GREEN → GoToColor RED
- Slightly different probability combos

Note: LED colors must be 0, 4, 5, or 6 only.

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 5

All 14 machines scored between -13.10 and -14.76 - very tight. The champion is:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2), 4 transitions: BLACK→s1 (0.95), GRAY→s1 (0.7), seeGREEN→s1 (0.6), WHITE→s1 (0.05)
- State 1: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), 3 transitions: WHITE→s0 (0.9), GRAY→s0 (0.3), seeRED→s0 (0.5)

Key insight: The 4th transition in state 0 is WHITE→s1 (p=0.05) in the champion - this means robots occasionally flee when detecting white floor (nest), avoiding getting stuck there. This beat the random (p=0.01) variant.

The rank 2 has state 1 with only 2 transitions (no camera), suggesting the camera in state 1 might not always help.

Rank 3 uses BLACK→s1 (0.95) as the 4th transition - also good (-14.40).

The whole population is very clustered. I need to:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: try WHITE transition p=0.03, 0.07, 0.1; try combining WHITE 4th transition with different GRAY p; try different camera p in state 0
3. Explore: try adding WHITE condition to state 1 as well; try different camera conditions; try slight topology changes

- p(seeGREEN in s0): 0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
